Parametricity for Primitive Nested Types and GADTs Thanks: Supported by NSF awards CCR-1906388 and CCR-1420175.
Patricia Johann \rsuper a, Enrico Ghiorzi \rsuper b

Abstract
. This paper considers parametricity and its consequent free theorems for nested data types. Rather than representing nested types via their Church encodings in a higher-kinded or dependently typed extension of System F, we adopt a functional programming perspective and design a Hindley-Milner-style calculus with primitives for constructing nested types directly as fixpoints. Our calculus can express all nested types appearing in the literature, including truly nested types. At the level of terms, it supports primitive pattern matching, map functions, and fold combinators for nested types. Our main contribution is the construction of a parametric model for our calculus. This is both delicate and challenging. In particular, to ensure the existence of semantic fixpoints interpreting nested types, and thus to establish a suitable Identity Extension Lemma for our calculus, our type system must explicitly track functoriality of types, and cocontinuity conditions on the functors interpreting them must be appropriately threaded throughout the model construction. We also prove that our model satisfies an appropriate Abstraction Theorem, as well as that it verifies all standard consequences of parametricity in the presence of primitive nested types. We give several concrete examples illustrating how our model can be used to derive useful free theorems, including a short cut fusion transformation, for programs over nested types. Finally, we consider generalizing our results to GADTs, and argue that no extension of our parametric model for nested types can give a functorial interpretation of GADTs in terms of left Kan extensions and still be parametric.



Jan. 14, 2021Dec. 23, 2021

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 17, Issue 4, Paper 23

1.
Introduction

Algebraic data types
(ADTs), both built-in and user-defined,
have long been at the core of functional languages such as Haskell,
ML, Agda, Epigram, and Idris. ADTs, such as that of natural numbers,
can be unindexed. But they can also be indexed over other types. For
example, the ADT of lists (here coded in Agda)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{data\;List\;(A:Set)\;:\;Set\;where}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{nil\;:\;List\;A}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{cons\;:\;A\rightarrow List\;A\rightarrow List\;A}\end{array}

is indexed over its element type
\mathtt{A}
. The instance of

\mathtt{List}
at index
\mathtt{A}
depends only on itself, and so is
independent of
\mathtt{List\,B}
for any other index
\mathtt{B}
.
That is,
\mathtt{List}
, like all other ADTs, defines a
family
of inductive types
, one for each index type.

Over time, there has been a notable trend toward data types whose
non-regular indexing can capture invariants and other sophisticated
properties that can be used for program verification and other
applications. A simple example of such a type is given by the type

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{data\;PTree\;(A:Set)\;:\;Set\;where}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{pleaf\;:\;A\rightarrow PTree\;A}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{pnode\;:\;PTree\;(A\times A)\rightarrow PTree\;A}\end{array}

of perfect trees, which can be thought of as constraining lists to
have lengths that are powers of 2. A similar data type

\mathtt{Nest}
is given in the canonical paper
[
BM98
]
on nested
types. The above code makes clear that perfect trees at index type

\mathtt{A}
are defined in terms of perfect trees at index type

\mathtt{A\times A}
. This is typical of nested types, one type
instance of which can depend on others, so that the entire family of
types must actually be defined at once. A nested type thus defines not
a family of inductive types, but rather an
inductive family of
types
. Nested types include simple nested types, like perfect
trees, none of whose recursive occurrences occur below another type
constructor; “deep” nested types
[
JP20
]
, such as the nested
type

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{data\;PForest\;(A:Set)\;:\;Set\;where}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{fempty\;:\;PForest\;A}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{fnode\;:\;A\rightarrow PTree\;(PForest\;A)\to PForest\;A}\end{array}

of perfect forests, whose recursive occurrences appear
below type constructors for other nested types; and truly nested
types, such as the nested type

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{data\;Bush\;(A:Set)\;:\;Set\;where}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{bnil\;:\;Bush\;A}\\
\hskip 28.90755pt\mathtt{bcons\;:\;A\rightarrow Bush\;(Bush\;A)\rightarrow Bush\;A}\end{array}

of bushes, whose recursive occurrences appear below
their own type constructors.

Relational parametricity
encodes a powerful notion of
type-uniformity, or representation independence, for data types in
polymorphic languages. It formalizes the intuition that a polymorphic
program must act uniformly on all of its possible type instantiations
by requiring that every such program preserves all relations between
pairs of types at which it is instantiated. Parametricity was
originally put forth by Reynolds
[
Rey83
]
for System
F
[
Gir72
]
, the calculus at the core of all polymorphic functional
languages. It was later popularized as Wadler’s “theorems for
free”
[
Wad89
]
, so called because it can deduce properties of
programs in such languages solely from their types, i.e., with no
knowledge whatsoever of the text of the programs involved. Most of
Wadler’s free theorems are consequences of naturality for polymorphic
list-processing functions. However, parametricity can also derive
results that go beyond just naturality, such as correctness for ADTs
of the program optimization known as
short cut
fusion

[
GLP93
,
Joh02
]
.

But what about nested types? Does parametricity still hold if such
types are added to polymorphic calculi? More practically, can we
justifiably reason type-independently about (functions over) nested
types in functional languages?

Type-independent reasoning about ADTs in functional languages is
usually justified by first representing ADTs by their Church
encodings, and then reasoning type-independently about these
encodings. This is typically justified by constructing a parametric
model — i.e, a model in which polymorphic functions preserve
relations
á la
Reynolds — for a suitable fragment of System
F, demonstrating that an initial algebra exists for the positive type
constructor corresponding to the functor underlying an ADT of
interest, and showing that each such initial algebra is suitably
isomorphic to its corresponding Church encoding. In fact, this
isomorphism of initial algebras and their Church encodings is one of
the “litmus tests” for the goodness of a parametric model.

This approach works well for ADTs, which are always fixpoints of
first-order
functors, and whose Church encodings, which involve
quantification over only type variables, are always expressible in
System F. For example,
\mathtt{List\,A}
is the fixpoint of the
first-order functor
F\,X=1+A\times X
and has Church encoding

\forall\alpha.\,\alpha\to(\mathtt{A}\to\alpha\to\alpha)\to\alpha
. But despite Cardelli’s
[
Car84
]
claim that “virtually
any basic type of interest can be encoded within F
2
” — i.e.,
within System F — non-ADT nested types cannot. Not even our
prototypical nested type of perfect trees has a Church encoding
expressible in System F! Indeed,
\mathtt{PTree\,A}
cannot be
represented as the fixpoint of any
first-order
functor. However,
it can be seen as the instance at index
\mathtt{A}
of the fixpoint
of the
higher-order
functor
H\,F\,A\,=\,(A\to F\,A)\to(F\,(A\times A)\to F\,A)\to F\,A
. It thus has Church encoding

\forall f.\,(\forall\alpha.\,\alpha\to f\alpha)\to(\forall\alpha.\,f(\alpha\times\alpha)\to f\alpha)\to\forall\alpha.\,f\alpha
, which requires quantification at the higher kind
*\to*

for
f
. A similar situation obtains for any (non-ADT) nested
type. Unfortunately, higher-kinded quantification is not available in
System F, so if we want to reason type-independently about nested
types in a language based on it we have only two options: (
i
) move to an extension of System F, such as the higher-kinded
calculus F
ω
or a dependent type theory, and reason via their
Church encodings in a known parametric model for that extension, or
(
ii
)  add nested types to System F as primitives — i.e., as
primitive type-level fixpoints — and construct a parametric model
for the result.

Since the type systems of F
ω
and dependent type theories are
designed to extend System F with far more than non-ADT data types, it
seems like serious overkill to pass to their parametric models to
reason about nested types in System F. Indeed, such calculi support
fundamentally new features — e.g., the full hierarchy of
higher-kinds or term-indexed types — adding complexity to their
models that is entirely unnecessary for reasoning about nested types.
In addition, to our knowledge no such models represent data types as
primitive fixpoints, so they cannot be used to obtain the results of
this paper. (See the last paragraph of this section for a more
detailed discussion in the case of F
ω
.) Whether or not these
results even hold for such calculi is an open question.

This paper therefore pursues the second option above. We first design
a Hindley-Milner-style calculus supporting primitive nested types,
together with primitive types of natural transformations representing
morphisms between them. Our calculus can express all nested types
appearing in the literature, including truly nested types. At the
term-level, it supports primitive pattern matching, map functions, and
fold combinators for nested types.
1
1

1

We leave incorporating
general term-level recursion to future work because, as
Pitts
[
Pit00
]
reminds us, “it is hard to construct models of
both impredicative polymorphism and fixpoint recursion”. In fact,
as the development in this paper shows, constructing a parametric
model even for our predicative calculus with primitive nested types
— and even without term-level fixpoints — is already rather
involved. On the other hand, our calculus is strongly normalizing,
so it perhaps edges us toward the kind of provably total practical
programming language proposed in
[
Wad89
]
.
Our main
contribution is the construction of a parametric model for our
calculus. This is both delicate and challenging. To ensure the
existence of semantic fixpoints interpreting nested types, and thus to
establish a suitable Identity Extension Lemma, our type system must
explicitly track functoriality of types, and cocontinuity conditions
on the functors interpreting them must be appropriately threaded
throughout the model construction. Our model validates all standard
consequences of parametricity in the presence of primitive nested
types, including the isomorphism of primitive ADTs and their Church
encodings, and correctness of short cut fusion for nested types. The
relationship between naturality and parametricity has long been of
interest, and our inclusion of a primitive type of natural
transformations allows us to clearly delineate those consequences of
parametricity that follow from naturality, from those, such as short
cut fusion for nested types, that require the full power of
parametricity.

Structure of this Paper
We introduce our calculus in
Section
2
. Its type system is based on the
level-2-truncation of the higher-kinded grammar from
[
JP19
]
,
augmented with a primitive type of natural
transformations. (Since
[
JP19
]
contains no term calculus, the
issue of parametricity could not even be raised there.) In
Section
3
we give set and relational
interpretations of our types. Set interpretations are possible
precisely because our calculus is predicative — as ensured by our
primitive natural transformation types — and
[
JP19
]
guarantees
that local finite presentability of
\mathsf{Set}
makes it suitable for
interpreting nested types. As is standard in categorical models,
types are interpreted as functors from environments interpreting their
type variable contexts to sets or relations, as appropriate. To ensure
that these functors satisfy the cocontinuity properties needed for the
semantic fixpoints interpreting nested types to exist, set environments
must map
k
-ary type constructor variables to appropriately
cocontinuous
k
-ary functors on sets, relation environments must map

k
-ary type constructor variables to appropriately cocontinuous

k
-ary relation transformers, and these cocontinuity conditions must
be threaded through our type interpretations in such a way that an
Identity Extension Lemma (Theorem
5
) can be
proved. Properly propagating the cocontinuity conditions requires
considerable care, and Section
4
, where it is done, is
(apart from tracking functoriality in the calculus so that it is
actually possible) where the bulk of the work in constructing our
model lies.

In Section
5
, we give set and relational
interpretations for the terms of our calculus. As usual in categorical
models, terms are interpreted as natural transformations from
interpretations of their term contexts to interpretations of their
types, and these must cohere in what is essentially a fibred way. In
Section
6.1
we prove a scheme deriving free
theorems that are consequences of naturality of polymorphic functions
over nested types. This scheme is very general, and is parameterized
over both the data type and the type of the polymorphic function at
hand. It has, for example, analogues for nested types of Wadler’s
map-rearrangement free theorems as instances. In
Section
6
we prove that our model satisfies an
Abstraction Theorem (Theorem
10
), which we use to
derive, in Section
7
, other parametricity results that go
beyond mere naturality. In Section
8
we show that the
results of this paper do not extend to arbitrary GADTs, and argue that
no extension of the parametric model we construct here for nested
types can give a functorial interpretation of GADTs in terms of left
Kan extensions as proposed in
[
JP19
]
and still be parametric. We
conclude and offer some directions for future work in
Section
9
.

This paper extends the conference paper
[
JGJ21
]
by including more
exposition, proofs of all theorems, and more examples. In addition,
the discussion of parametricity for GADTs in Section
8

is entirely new.

Related Work
There is a long line of work on categorical
models of parametricity for System F; see,
e.g.,
[
BFSS90
,
BM05
,
DR04
,
GJF+15
,
Has94
,
Jac99
,
MR92
,
RR94
]
. To our
knowledge, all such models treat ADTs via their Church encodings,
verifying in the just-constructed parametric model that each Church
encoding is a solution for the fixpoint equation associated with its
corresponding data type. The present paper draws on this rich
tradition of categorical models of parametricity for System F, but
modifies them to treat nested types (and therefore ADTs) as primitive
data types.

The only other extensions we know of System F with primitive data
types are those in
[
Mat11
,
MG01
,
Pit98
,
Pit00
,
Wad89
]
.
Wadler
[
Wad89
]
treats full System F, and sketches parametricity
for its extension with lists. Martin and Gibbons
[
MG01
]
outline a
semantics for a grammar of primitive nested types similar to that
in
[
JP19
]
, but treat only polynomial nested types, i.e., nested
types that are fixpoints of polynomial higher-order
functors. Unfortunately, the model suggested in
[
MG01
]
is not
entirely correct (see
[
JP19
]
), and parametricity is nowhere
mentioned. Matthes
[
Mat11
]
treats System F with non-polynomial
ADTs and nested types, but his focus is on expressivity of generalized
Mendler iteration for them. He gives no semantics whatsoever.

In
[
Pit00
]
, Pitts adds list ADTs to full System F with a
term-level fixpoint primitive. Other ADTs are included
in
[
Pit98
]
, but nested types are not expressible in either
syntax. Pitts constructs parametric models for his calculi based on
operational, rather than categorical, semantics. A benefit of using
operational semantics to build parametric models is that it avoids
needing to work in a suitable metatheory to accommodate System F’s
impredicativity. It is well-known that there are no set-based
parametric models of System F
[
Rey84
]
, so parametric models for
it and its extensions are often constructed in a syntactic metatheory
such as the impredicative Calculus of Inductive Constructions (iCIC).
By adding primitive nested types to a Hindley-Milner-style calculus
and working in a categorical setting we side-step such metatheoretic
distractions.

Atkey
[
Atk12
]
treats parametricity for arbitrary higher kinds,
constructing a parametric model for System F
ω
within iCIC,
rather than in a semantic category. His construction is in some ways
similar to ours, but he represents (now higher-kinded) data types
using Church encodings rather than as primitives. Moreover, the

\mathit{fmap}
functions associated to Atkey’s functors must be
given
, presumably by the programmer, together with their underlying
type constructors. This absolves him of imposing cocontinuity
conditions on his model to ensure that fixpoints of his functors
exist, but, unfortunately, he does not indicate which type
constructors support
\mathit{fmap}
functions. We suspect explicitly
spelling out which types can be interpreted as strictly positive
functors would result in a full higher-kinded extension of a calculus
akin to that presented here.

2.
The Calculus

2.1.
Types

For each
k\geq 0
, we assume countable sets
\mathbb{T}^{k}
of
type
constructor variables of arity
k
and
\mathbb{F}^{k}
of

functorial variables of arity
k
, all mutually disjoint. The
sets of all type constructor variables and functorial variables are

\mathbb{T}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{T}^{k}
and
\mathbb{F}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{F}^{k}
, respectively, and a
type variable
is any element of

\mathbb{T}\cup\mathbb{F}
. We use lower case Greek letters for type
variables, writing
\phi^{k}
to indicate that
\phi\in\mathbb{T}^{k}\cup\mathbb{F}^{k}
, and omitting the arity indicator
k
when convenient,
unimportant, or clear from context. Letters from the beginning of the
alphabet denote type variables of arity
0
, i.e., elements of

\mathbb{T}^{0}\cup\mathbb{F}^{0}
. We write
\overline{\phi}
for either a set

\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\}
of type constructor variables or a set of
functorial variables when the cardinality
n
of the set is
unimportant or clear from context. If
V
is a set of type variables
we write
V,\overline{\phi}
for
V\cup\overline{\phi}
when
V\cap\overline{\phi}=\emptyset
. We omit the vector notation for a
singleton set, thus writing
\phi
, instead of
\overline{\phi}
, for

\{\phi\}
.

If
\Gamma
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{T}
,
\Phi
is a finite
subset of
\mathbb{F}
,
\overline{\alpha}
is a finite subset of

\mathbb{F}^{0}
disjoint from
\Phi
, and
\phi^{k}\in\mathbb{F}^{k}\setminus\Phi
, then the set
\mathcal{F}
of well-formed types is given in
Definition
2.1
. The notation there entails that an
application
FF_{1}\dots F_{k}
is allowed only when
F
is a type variable
of arity
k
, or
F
is a subexpression of the form
\mu\phi^{k}.\lambda\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}.F^{\prime}
. Accordingly, an overbar
indicates a sequence of types whose length matches the arity of the
type applied to it. Requiring that types are always in such

\eta
-long normal form
avoids having to consider

\beta
-conversion at the level of types. In a type

\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G
, the
\mathsf{Nat}
operator binds all occurrences of
the variables in
\overline{\alpha}
in
F
and
G
; intuitively,

\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G
represents the type of a natural
transformation in
\overline{\alpha}
from the functor
F
to the functor

G
. In a subexpression
\mu\phi^{k}.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.F
, the
\mu

operator binds all occurrences of the variable
\phi
, and the

\lambda
operator binds all occurrences of the variables in

\overline{\alpha}
, in the body
F
.

A
type constructor context
, or
non-functorial context
, is
a finite set
\Gamma
of type constructor variables, and a
functorial context
is a finite set
\Phi
of functorial
variables. In Definition
2.1
, a judgment of the form

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F
indicates that the type
F
is intended to be
functorial in the variables in
\Phi
but not necessarily in those in

\Gamma
.

{defi}

The formation rules for the set
\mathcal{F}
of
(well-formed) types

are

\Gamma,\Phi

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{0}

\Gamma,\Phi

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{1}

\Gamma;\overline{\alpha^{0}}\vdash F

\Gamma;\overline{\alpha^{0}}\vdash G

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha^{0}}}F\,G

\phi^{k}\in\Gamma\cup\Phi

\quad\quad\overline{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi^{k}\overline{F}

\Gamma;\overline{\alpha^{0}},\phi^{k}\vdash F

\quad\quad\overline{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi^{k}.\lambda\overline{\alpha^{0}}.\,F)\,\overline{G}

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G

\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F\times G

We write
\vdash F
for
\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash F
.
Definition
2.1
ensures that the expected weakening rules
for well-formed types hold, i.e., if
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F
is
well-formed, then both
\Gamma,\overline{\psi};\Phi\vdash F
and
\Gamma;\Phi,\overline{\phi}\vdash F
are also well-formed. Note that weakening
does not change the contexts in which types can be formed. For
example, a
\mathsf{Nat}
-type — i.e., a type of the form

\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G
— can be formed in any functorial
context. But since
\mathsf{Nat}
-types contain no functorial variables, we
will form them in empty functorial contexts below whenever
convenient. Of course, we cannot
first
weaken the functorial
contexts of
F
and
G
to
\Gamma;\overline{\alpha},\beta\vdash F
and

\Gamma;\overline{\alpha},\beta\vdash G
, and
then
form

\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G
in the weakened context
\Gamma;\beta
.
If
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash F
and
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash G
, then
our rules allow formation of the type
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}F\,G
, which represents the System F type
\Gamma\vdash F\to G
. Similarly, if
\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F
, then our
rules allow formation of the type
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,\mathbb{1}\,F
, which represents the System F type

\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\forall\overline{\alpha}.F
. However, some System
F types, such as
\forall\alpha.(\alpha\to\alpha)\to\alpha
, are
not representable in our calculus. Note that, in the rule for

\mu
-types, no variables in
\Phi
appear in the body
F
of
\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.F
. This will be critical to proving the
Identity Extension Lemma for our calculus.

Definition
2.1
allows the formation of all of the types
from Section
1
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathit{List}\,\alpha&=&\mu\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\beta\;\mbox{ or }\;(\mu\phi.\lambda\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi\beta)\,\alpha\\
\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha&=&(\mu\phi.\lambda\beta.\,\beta+\phi\,(\beta\times\beta))\,\alpha\\
\mathit{Forest}\,\alpha&=&(\mu\phi.\lambda\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\mathit{PTree}\,(\phi\beta))\,\alpha\\
\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha&=&(\mu\phi.\lambda\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi\,(\phi\beta))\,\alpha\end{array}

Note that since the body
F
of a type
(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.F)\overline{G}
can only be functorial in
\phi
and the
variables in
\overline{\alpha}
, the representation of

\mathit{List}\,\alpha
as the ADT
\mu\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\beta
cannot be functorial in
\alpha
. By contrast, if

\mathit{List}\,\alpha
is represented as the nested type
(\mu\phi.\lambda\beta.\,\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi\beta)\,\alpha
then
we can choose
\alpha
to be a functorial variable or not when forming
the type. This observation holds for other ADTs as well; for example,
if
\mathit{Tree}\,\alpha\,\gamma=\mu\beta.\alpha+\beta\times\gamma\times\beta
, then
\alpha,\gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathit{Tree}\,\alpha\,\gamma
is well-formed, but
\emptyset;\alpha,\gamma\vdash\mathit{Tree}\,\alpha\,\gamma
is not. And it also
applies to some non-ADT types, such as
\mathit{GRose}\,\phi\,\alpha=\mu\beta.\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\phi\beta
, in which
\phi
and

\alpha
must both be non-functorial variables. It is in fact
possible to allow “extra”
0
-ary functorial variables in the body
of
\mu
-types (functorial variables of higher arity are the real
problem), which would allow the first-order representations of ADTs to
be functorial. However, since doing this requires some changes to the
formation rule for
\mu
-types, as well as the delicate threading of
some additional conditions throughout our model construction, we do
not pursue this line of investigation here.

Definition
2.1
allows well-formed types to be functorial
in no variables. Functorial variables can also be demoted to
non-functorial status: if
F[\phi:==\psi]
is the textual
replacement of
\phi
in
F
, then
\Gamma,\psi^{k};\Phi\vdash F[\phi^{k}:==\psi^{k}]
is derivable whenever
\Gamma;\Phi,\phi^{k}\vdash F
is. The proof is by induction on the structure of
F
. In
addition to textual replacement, we also have a substitution operation
on types.

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F
is a type, if
\Gamma
and
\Phi
contain
only type variables of arity
0
, and if
k=0
for every occurrence of

\phi^{k}
bound by
\mu
in
F
, then we say that
F
is
first-order
; otherwise we say that
F
is
second-order
. Substitution for first-order types is the usual
capture-avoiding textual substitution. We write
H[\alpha:=F]

for the result of substituting
F
for
\alpha
in
H
, and

H[\alpha_{1}:=F_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}:=F_{k}]
, or
H[\overline{\alpha:=F}]

when convenient, for
H[\alpha_{1}:=F_{1}][\alpha_{2}:=F_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}:=F_{k}]
. The operation of
second-order type substitution along

\overline{\alpha}
is given in Definition
2.1
,
where we adopt a similar notational convention for vectors of types.
Of course,
(\cdot)[\phi^{0}:=_{\emptyset}F]
coincides with first-order
substitution. We omit
\overline{\alpha}
when convenient, but note that it
is not correct to substitute along non-functorial variables.

{defi}

If
\Gamma;\Phi,\phi^{k}\vdash H
and
\Gamma;\Phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash F
with
|\overline{\alpha}|=k
, then the operation
H[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]
of
second-order type substitution along

\overline{\alpha}
is defined by induction on
H
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbb{0}[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&\mathbb{0}\\[2.15277pt]
\mathbb{1}[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&\mathbb{1}\\[1.07639pt]
(\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta}}G\,K)[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta}}\,G\,K\\
(\psi\overline{G})[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\psi\,\overline{G[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]}&\mbox{if }\psi\not=\phi\\
F[\overline{\alpha:=G[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]}]&\mbox{if }\psi=\phi\end{array}\right.\\[12.05553pt]
(G+K)[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&G[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]+K[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]\\[2.15277pt]
(G\times K)[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&G[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]\times K[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]\\[2.15277pt]
((\mu\psi.\lambda\overline{\beta}.G)\overline{K})[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]&=&(\mu\psi.\lambda\overline{\beta}.\,G)\,\overline{K[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]}\end{array}

The idea is that the arguments to
\phi
get substituted for the
variables in
\overline{\alpha}
in each
F
replacing an occurrence of

\phi
. It is not hard to see that
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash H[\phi:=_{\overline{\alpha}}F]
.

2.2.
Terms

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hskip 14.45377pt\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 28.51369pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to102.43004pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta,x:F\vdash x:F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:\mathbb{0}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to130.77278pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 22.5556pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\bot_{F}t:F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.41666pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 22.6388pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\phantom{\Gamma;\Phi}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to69.52763pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\top:\mathbb{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\\
\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{cc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hskip 57.81621pt\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 14.459pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash s:F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to104.0079pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{inl}\,s:F+G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 14.95139pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to103.95932pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{inr}\,t:F+G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\\
\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\;\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hskip-14.45377pt\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F,G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:F+G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta,x:F\vdash l:K\hskip 14.45377pt\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta,y:G\vdash r:K$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to389.82446pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 120.78276pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi~|~\Delta\vdash\mathsf{case}\,{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{{x\mapsto l};\,{y\mapsto r}\}:K$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lll}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash s:F$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to163.60025pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 26.29619pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash(s,t):F\times G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:F\times G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to94.09816pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\enskip\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\pi_{1}t:F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:F\times G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to94.09816pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\enskip\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\pi_{2}t:G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\,|\,\Delta,x:F\vdash t:G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to202.90283pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 38.50296pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash L_{\overline{\alpha}}x.t:\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\\
\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 15.65334pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\overline{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash K}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\emptyset\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash s:F[\overline{\alpha:=K}]$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to228.76248pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 66.98141pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash t_{\overline{K}}s:G[\overline{\alpha:=K}]$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\\
\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 48.27197pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\overline{\phi},\overline{\gamma}\vdash H$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\overline{\Gamma;\overline{\beta},\overline{\gamma}\vdash F}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\overline{\Gamma;\overline{\beta},\overline{\gamma}\vdash G}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to199.27092pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi~|~\Delta\vdash\mathsf{map}^{\overline{F},\overline{G}}_{H}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\;(\overline{\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta},\overline{\gamma}}\,F\,G})\;(\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\gamma}}\,H[\overline{\phi:=_{\overline{\beta}}F}]\;H[\overline{\phi:=_{\overline{\beta}}G}])$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\\
\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hskip 14.45377pt\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 99.42892pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to249.30223pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{in}_{H}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta}}H[\phi:=_{\overline{\beta}}(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{\beta}][\overline{\alpha:=\beta}]\,(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{\beta}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\\
\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hskip 14.45377pt\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 95.39072pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\overline{\beta}\vdash F$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to294.41565pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\Phi\,|\,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{fold}^{F}_{H}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\;(\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta}}\,H[\phi:=_{\overline{\beta}}F][\overline{\alpha:=\beta}]\,F)\;(\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\beta}}\,(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{\beta}\,F)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Well-formed terms

To define our term calculus we assume an infinite set
\cal V
of term
variables disjoint from
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{F}
. If
\Gamma
is a type
constructor context and
\Phi
is a functorial context, then a
term context for
\Gamma
and
\Phi
is a finite set of bindings of
the form
x:F
, where
x\in{\cal V}
and
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F
. We adopt the above conventions for denoting disjoint unions and
vectors in term contexts. If
\Delta
is a term context for
\Gamma

and
\Phi
, then the formation rules for the set of
well-formed
terms over
\Delta
are as in Figure
1
. In the rule
there for
L_{\overline{\alpha}}x.t
, the
L
operator binds all occurrences
of the type variables in
\overline{\alpha}
in the types of
x
and
t
, as
well as all occurrences of
x
in
t
. In the rule for
t_{\overline{K}}s

there is one type in
\overline{K}
for every functorial variable in
\overline{\alpha}
. In the rule for
\mathsf{map}^{\overline{F},\overline{G}}_{H}
there is one type

F
and one type
G
for each functorial variable in

\overline{\phi}
. Moreover, for each
\phi^{k}
in
\overline{\phi}
the number of
functorial variables in
\overline{\beta}
in the judgments for its
corresponding type
F
and
G
is
k
. In the rules for
\mathsf{in}_{H}
and

\mathsf{fold}^{F}_{H}
, the functorial variables in
\overline{\beta}
are fresh with
respect to
H
, and there is one
\beta
for every

\alpha
. Substitution for terms is the obvious extension of the usual
capture-avoiding textual substitution, and the rules of
Figure
1
ensure that weakening is respected. Below we
form
L
-terms, which contain no free functorial variables, in empty
functorial contexts whenever convenient. We similarly form
\mathsf{map}
-,

\mathsf{in}
-, and
\mathsf{fold}
-terms, which contain no free functorial variables
or free term variables, in empty functorial contexts and empty term
contexts whenever convenient.

The “extra” functorial variables
\overline{\gamma}
in the rules for

\mathsf{map}^{\overline{F},\overline{G}}_{H}
(i.e., those variables not affected by the
substitution of
\phi
) deserve comment. They allow us to map
polymorphic functions over nested types. Suppose, for example, we want
to map the polymorphic function
\mathit{flatten}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\beta}(\mathit{PTree}\,\beta)\,(\mathit{List}\,\beta)
over a list. Even in
the absence of extra variables the instance of
\mathsf{map}
required to map
each non-functorial monomorphic instantiation of
\mathit{flatten}

over a list of perfect trees is well-formed:

\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-7.22743pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 98.52017pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\alpha\vdash\mathit{List}\,\alpha$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathit{PTree}\,F$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathit{List}\,G$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to411.23976pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\emptyset\,|\,\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{map}^{\mathit{PTree}\,F,\,\mathit{List}\,G}_{\mathit{List}\,\alpha}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\,(\mathit{PTree}\,F)\,(\mathit{List}\,G))\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{PTree}\,F))\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{List}\,G)))$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

But in the absence of
\overline{\gamma}
, the instance

\Gamma;\emptyset~|~\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{map}^{\mathit{PTree}\,\beta,\mathit{List}\,\beta}_{\mathit{List}\,\alpha}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathsf{Nat}^{\beta}(\mathit{PTree}\,\beta)\,(\mathit{List}\,\beta))\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\beta}\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\beta))\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{List}\,\beta)))

required to map the
polymorphic

\mathit{flatten}

function over a list of perfect trees is not: indeed, the functorial
contexts for
F
and
G
in the rule for
\mathsf{map}^{F,G}_{H}
would have to
be empty, but because the polymorphic
\mathit{flatten}
function is
natural in
\beta
it cannot possibly have a type of the form

\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}F\,G
as would be required for it to be the function
input to
\mathsf{map}
. Untypeability of this instance of
\mathsf{map}
is
unsatisfactory in a polymorphic calculus, where we naturally expect to
be able to manipulate entire polymorphic functions rather than just
their monomorphic instances, but the “extra” variables
\overline{\gamma}

remedy the situation, ensuring that the instance of
\mathsf{map}
needed to
map the polymorphic
\mathit{flatten}
function is typeable as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 89.7987pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\alpha,\gamma\vdash\mathit{List}\,\alpha$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\gamma\vdash\mathit{PTree}\,\gamma\hskip 21.68121pt\Gamma;\gamma\vdash\mathit{List}\,\gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}\hbox to397.67323pt{\leaders{\hrule height=0.8pt}{\hfill}}\lower-0.4pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma;\emptyset~|~\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{map}^{\mathit{PTree}\,\gamma,\mathit{List}\,\gamma}_{\mathit{List}\,\alpha}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathsf{Nat}^{\gamma}(\mathit{PTree}\,\gamma)\,(\mathit{List}\,\gamma))\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\gamma}\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\gamma))\,(\mathit{List}\,(\mathit{List}\,\gamma)))$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

Our calculus is expressive enough to define a function

\mathit{reversePTree:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)}

that reverses the order of the leaves in a perfect tree. This function
maps, e.g., the perfect tree

\mathit{pnode}\,(\mathit{pnode}\,(\mathit{pleaf}\,((1,2),(3,4))))

to the perfect tree

\mathit{pnode}\,(\mathit{pnode}\,(\mathit{pleaf}\,((4,3),(2,1))))

i.e., maps
((1,2),(3,4))

to
((4,3),(2,1))
. It can be defined as

\vdash(\mathsf{fold}_{\beta+\phi(\beta\times\beta)}^{\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha})_{\emptyset}\,s:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)

where

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{fold}_{\beta+\phi(\beta\times\beta)}^{\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha}&:&\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\alpha+\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha))\;(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha))\;(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\;(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha))\\
\mathsf{in}_{\beta+\phi(\beta\times\beta)}&:&\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\alpha+\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha))\;(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\\
\mathsf{map}_{\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha}^{\alpha\times\alpha,\alpha\times\alpha}&:&\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\alpha\times\alpha)\,(\alpha\times\alpha))\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha))\,(\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha)))\end{array}

and

\mathit{pleaf}
,
\mathit{pnode}
,
\mathit{swap}
, and
s
are the terms

\begin{array}[]{l}\vdash\mathsf{in}_{\beta+\phi(\beta\times\beta)}\circ(L_{\alpha}x.\,\mathsf{inl}\,x)\;:\;\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,\alpha\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\\
\vdash\mathsf{in}_{\beta+\phi(\beta\times\beta)}\circ(L_{\alpha}x.\,\mathsf{inr}\,x)\;:\;\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,\mathit{PTree}(\alpha\times\alpha)\,(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\\
\vdash L_{\alpha}p.\,(\pi_{2}p,\pi_{1}p):\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\alpha\times\alpha)\,(\alpha\times\alpha)\\
\vdash L_{\alpha}t.\,\mathsf{case}\,{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{{b\mapsto\mathit{pleaf}\,b};\,{t^{\prime}\mapsto\mathit{pnode}\,(((\mathsf{map}_{\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha}^{\alpha\times\alpha,\alpha\times\alpha})_{\emptyset}\,\mathit{swap})_{\alpha}\,t^{\prime})}\}\\
\hskip 36.135pt:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\alpha+\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha))\;\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha\end{array}

respectively. Here, if
\Gamma;\emptyset\,|\,\Delta\vdash t:\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G
and
\Gamma;\emptyset\,|\,\Delta\vdash s:\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}G\,H
are terms then the
composition

s\circ t
of
t
and
s
is defined by
s\circ t=\Gamma;\emptyset\,|\,\Delta\vdash L_{\overline{\alpha}}x.s_{\overline{\alpha}}(t_{\overline{\alpha}}x):\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,H
. Our calculus can similarly define a

\mathtt{rustle}
function that changes the placement of the data in a
bush. This function maps, e.g., the bush

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{bcons}\,0\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,1\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,2\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\\
\hskip 67.21056pt(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,3\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,4\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\end{array}

to the bush

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-118.52275pt\mathit{bcons}\,4\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,0\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,3\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\\
\hskip 67.21056pt(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,2\,(\mathit{bcons}\,(\mathit{bcons}\,1\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\,\mathit{bnil})\,\mathit{bnil}))\end{array}

It can be defined as

\vdash(\mathsf{fold}^{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)})_{\emptyset}\,balg:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)

where

\begin{array}[]{lll}\vdash\mathsf{fold}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}^{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\mathit{Bush}\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)))\;(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\;(\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\;(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\\
\end{array}

and
\mathit{bnil}
,
\mathit{bcons}
,
\mathsf{in}^{-1}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}
,
\mathit{balg}
, and
\mathit{consalg}

are the terms

\begin{array}[]{l}\vdash\mathsf{in}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}\circ(L_{\alpha}\,x.\,\mathsf{inl}\,x)\;:\;\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,\mathbb{1}\;(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\\
\vdash\mathsf{in}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}\circ(L_{\alpha}\,x.\,\mathsf{inr}\,x)\;:\;\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\alpha\times\mathit{Bush}\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\;(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\\[3.61371pt]
\vdash(\mathsf{fold}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}^{(\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta))[\phi:=\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha]})_{\emptyset}\,((\mathsf{map}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)}^{(\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta))[\phi:=\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha][\beta:=\alpha],\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha})_{\emptyset}\,\mathsf{in}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)})\\[3.61371pt]
\hskip 14.45377pt:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\;(\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\mathit{Bush}\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\\
\vdash L_{\alpha}\,s.\mathsf{case}\,{s}\,\mathsf{of}\,\{{*\mapsto\mathit{bnil}_{\alpha}*};\,{(a,bba)\mapsto consalg_{\alpha}(a,bba)}\}:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\mathit{Bush}(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\\
\vdash L_{\alpha}\,(a,bba).\mathsf{case}\,((\mathsf{in}^{-1}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)})_{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}\,bba)\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\\
\hskip 80.00012pt\,*\mapsto\mathit{bcons}_{\alpha}(a,\mathit{bnil}_{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}*);\\
\hskip 80.00012pt(ba,bbba)\mapsto\mathsf{case}\,((\mathsf{in}^{-1}_{\mathbb{1}+\beta\times\phi(\phi\beta)})_{\alpha}\,ba)\,\mathsf{of}\,\{\\
\hskip 160.00024pt*\mapsto\mathit{bcons}_{\alpha}(a,\mathit{bcons}_{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}(\mathit{bnil}_{\alpha}*,bbba));\\
\hskip 160.00024pt(a^{\prime},bba^{\prime})\mapsto\mathit{bcons}_{\alpha}(a^{\prime},\mathit{bcons}_{\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha}(\mathit{bcons}_{\alpha}(a,bba^{\prime}),bbba))\}\!\}\\
\hskip 14.45377pt:\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}\,(\alpha\times\mathit{Bush}(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha))\,(\mathit{Bush}\,\alpha)\\
\end{array}

respectively.

Unfortunately, our calculus cannot express types of recursive
functions — such as a concatenation function for perfect trees or a
zip function for bushes — that take as inputs a nested type and an
argument of another type, both of which are parameterized over the
same variable. The fundamental issue is that recursion is expressible
only via
\mathsf{fold}
, which produces natural transformations in some
variables
\overline{\alpha}
from
\mu
-types to other functors
F
. The
restrictions on
\mathsf{Nat}
-types entail that
F
cannot itself be a

\mathsf{Nat}
-type containing
\overline{\alpha}
, so, e.g.,
\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\,(\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha)))
is not well-formed.
Uncurrying gives
\mathsf{Nat}^{\alpha}(\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha\times\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha)\,(\mathit{PTree}\,(\alpha\times\alpha))
,
which is well-formed, but
\mathsf{fold}
cannot produce a term of this
type because
\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha\times\mathit{PTree}\,\alpha
is
not a
\mu
-type. Our calculus can, however, express types of
recursive functions that take multiple nested types as arguments,
provided they are parameterized over disjoint sets of type variables
and the return type of the function is parameterized over only the
variables occurring in the type of its final argument. Even for ADTs
there is a difference between which folds over them we can type when
they are viewed as ADTs (i.e., as fixpoints of first-order functors)
versus as proper nested types (i.e., as fixpoints of higher-order
functors). This is because, in the return type of
\mathsf{fold}
, the
arguments of the
\mu
-type must be variables bound by
\mathsf{Nat}
. For
ADTs, the
\mu
-type takes no arguments, making it possible to write
recursive functions, such as a concatenation function for lists of
type
\alpha;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}\ (\mu\beta.\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\beta)\,(\mathsf{Nat}^{\emptyset}(\mu\beta.\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\beta)\,(\mu\beta.\mathbb{1}+\alpha\times\beta))
. This is
not possible for nested types — even when they are semantically
equivalent to ADTs.

Interestingly, even some recursive functions of a single proper nested
type — e.g., a reverse function for bushes that is a true involution
— cannot be expressed as folds because the algebra arguments needed
to define them are again recursive functions with types of the same
problematic form as the type of, e.g., a zip function for perfect
trees. Expressivity of folds for nested types has long been a vexing
issue, and this is naturally inherited by our calculus. Adding more
expressive recursion combinators could help, but since this is
orthogonal to the issue of parametricity in the presence of primitive
nested types we do not consider it further here.

3.
Interpreting Types

We denote the category of sets and functions by
\mathsf{Set}
. The category

\mathsf{Rel}
has as its objects triples
(A,B,R)
where
R
is a relation
between the objects
A
and
B
in
\mathsf{Set}
, i.e., a subset of
A\times B
, and has as its morphisms from
(A,B,R)
to
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime},R^{\prime})
pairs
(f:A\to A^{\prime},g:B\to B^{\prime})
of morphisms in
\mathsf{Set}
such that
(fa,g\,b)\in R^{\prime}
whenever
(a,b)\in R
. We write
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
in place of

(A,B,R)
when convenient. If
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
we write
\pi_{1}R
and

\pi_{2}R
for the
domain

A
of
R
and the
codomain

B

of
R
, respectively. If
A:\mathsf{Set}
, then we write
\mathsf{Eq}_{A}=(A,A,\{(x,x)~|~x\in A\})
for the
equality relation
on
A
.

The key idea underlying Reynolds’ parametricity is to give each type

F(\alpha)
with one free variable
\alpha
both an
object
interpretation

F_{0}
taking sets to sets and a
relational
interpretation

F_{1}
taking relations
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
to relations

F_{1}(R):\mathsf{Rel}(F_{0}(A),F_{0}(B))
, and to interpret each term

t(\alpha,x):F(\alpha)
with one free term variable
x:G(\alpha)

as a map
t_{0}
associating to each set
A
a function
t_{0}(A):G_{0}(A)\to F_{0}(A)
. These interpretations are to be given inductively on the
structures of
F
and
t
in such a way that they imply two
fundamental theorems. The first is an
Identity Extension Lemma
,
which states that
F_{1}(\mathsf{Eq}_{A})=\mathsf{Eq}_{F_{0}(A)}
, and is the essential
property that makes a model relationally parametric rather than just
induced by a logical relation. The second is an
Abstraction
Theorem
, which states that, for any
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
,

(t_{0}(A),t_{0}(B))
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Rel}
from

(G_{0}(A),G_{0}(B),G_{1}(R))
to
(F_{0}(A),F_{0}(B),F_{1}(R))
. The Identity
Extension Lemma is similar to the Abstraction Theorem except that it
holds for
all
elements of a type’s interpretation, not just
those that are interpretations of terms. Similar theorems are
expected to hold for types and terms with any number of free
variables.

The key to proving the Identity Extension Lemma in our setting
(Theorem
5
) is a familiar “cutting down” of the
interpretations of universally quantified types to include only the
“parametric” elements; the relevant types in our calculus are the

\mathsf{Nat}
-types. This cutting down requires, as usual, that the set
interpretations of types (Section
3.1
) are defined
simultaneously with their relational interpretations
(Section
3.2
). While the set interpretations are
relatively straightforward, their relation interpretations are less
so, mainly because of the cocontinuity conditions required to ensure
that they are well-defined. We develop these conditions in
Sections
3.1
and
3.2
. This separates
our set and relational interpretations in space, but otherwise has no
impact on the fact that they are given by mutual induction.

3.1.
Interpreting Types as Sets

We interpret types in our calculus as
\omega
-cocontinuous functors
on locally finitely presentable categories
[
AR94
]
. Both
\mathsf{Set}

and
\mathsf{Rel}
are locally finitely presentable categories. Since functor
categories of locally finitely presentable categories are again
locally finitely presentable, the fixpoints interpreting
\mu
-types
in
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{Rel}
must all exist, and thus the set and relational
interpretations of all of the types in Definition
2.1
,
are well-defined
[
JP19
]
. To bootstrap this process, we interpret
type variables as
\omega
-cocontinuous functors in
Definitions
3.1
and
3.2
. If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}

are locally finitely presentable categories, we write
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]
for
the category of
\omega
-cocontinuous functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
.

{defi}

A
set environment
maps each type variable in
\mathbb{T}^{k}\cup\mathbb{F}^{k}
to an element of
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
. A morphism
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
for set environments
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
with
\rho|_{\mathbb{T}}=\rho^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{T}}
maps each type constructor variable
\psi^{k}\in\mathbb{T}

to the identity natural transformation on
\rho\psi^{k}=\rho^{\prime}\psi^{k}

and each functorial variable
\phi^{k}\in\mathbb{F}
to a natural
transformation from the
k
-ary functor
\rho\phi^{k}
on
\mathsf{Set}
to the

k
-ary functor
\rho^{\prime}\phi^{k}
on
\mathsf{Set}
. Composition of morphisms on
set environments is given componentwise, with the identity morphism
mapping each set environment to itself. This gives a category of set
environments and morphisms between them, denoted
\mathsf{SetEnv}
.

When convenient we identify a functor in
[\mathsf{Set}^{0},\mathsf{Set}]
with its
value on
\ast
and consider a set environment to map a type variable
of arity
0
to a set. If
\overline{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\}

and
\overline{A}=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{k}\}
, then we write
\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]

for the set environment
\rho^{\prime}
such that
\rho^{\prime}\alpha_{i}=A_{i}
for

i=1,\dots,k
and
\rho^{\prime}\alpha=\rho\alpha
if
\alpha\not\in\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\}
. We note that
\lambda\overline{A}.\,\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]
is a functor in
\overline{A}
.

We can now define our set interpretation. Its action on objects of

\mathsf{SetEnv}
is given in Definition
3.1
, and its action on
morphisms of
\mathsf{SetEnv}
is given in Definition
3.1
.
If
\rho
is a set environment we write
\mathsf{Eq}_{\rho}
for the equality
relation environment such that
\mathsf{Eq}_{\rho}\phi=\mathsf{Eq}_{\rho\phi}
for
every type variable
\phi
; see Definitions
3.2

and
3.2
for the definitions of a relation transformer
and a relation environment, and Equation
10
for the
definition of the relation transformer
\mathsf{Eq}_{F}
on a functor
F
.
Equality relation environments appear in the third clause of
Definition
3.1
. The relational interpretation also
appearing in the third clause of Definition
3.1
is
given in Definition
3.2
.

{defi}

The
set interpretation

\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}:\mathcal{F}\to[\mathsf{SetEnv},\mathsf{Set}]

is defined by:

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{0}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{1}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=\{\eta:\lambda\overline{A}.\,\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]\Rightarrow\lambda\overline{A}.\,\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt|~\forall\overline{A},\overline{B}:\mathsf{Set}.\forall\overline{R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)}.

\displaystyle\hskip 28.90755pt(\eta_{\overline{A}},\eta_{\overline{B}}):\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Rel}}\mathsf{Eq}_{\rho}[\overline{\alpha:=R}]\rightarrow\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Rel}}\mathsf{Eq}_{\rho}[\overline{\alpha:=R}]\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=(\rho\phi)\,\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho+\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F\times G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho\times\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{G}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho

\displaystyle=(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}

\displaystyle\text{where }T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}\,F

\displaystyle=\lambda\overline{A}.\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\phi:=F][\overline{\alpha:=A}]

\displaystyle\text{and }T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}\,\eta

\displaystyle=\lambda\overline{A}.\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\mathit{id}_{\rho}[\phi:=\eta][\overline{\alpha:=\mathit{id}_{A}}]

If
\rho\in\mathsf{SetEnv}
and
\vdash F
then we write
\llbracket{\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}

instead of
\llbracket{\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho
since the environment is
immaterial. The third clause of Definition
3.1
does
indeed define a set: local finite presentability of
\mathsf{Set}
and

\omega
-cocontinuity of
\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}

ensure that
\{\eta:\lambda\overline{A}.\,\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]

\Rightarrow\lambda\overline{A}.\,\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=A}]\}

(containing
\llbracket{\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho
) can be embedded in the set

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{S}=(S_{1},\dots,S_{|\overline{\alpha}|})\\
S_{1},\dots,S_{|\overline{\alpha}|}\mbox{ are
finite cardinals}\end{subarray}}\hskip-28.90755pt(\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=S}])^{(\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho[\overline{\alpha:=S}])}

Also,
\llbracket{\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}F\,G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
is
\omega
-cocontinuous since it is
constant (in particular, on
\omega
-directed sets). This is because
Definition
3.1
ensures that restrictions to

\mathbb{T}^{k}
of morphisms between set environments are identities.
Interpretations of
\mathsf{Nat}
-types ensure that the interpretations

\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash F\to G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
and
\llbracket{\Gamma\vdash\forall\overline{\alpha}.F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
of the System F arrow types and
\forall
-types
representable in our calculus are as expected in any parametric model.

To make sense of the last clause in Definition
3.1
, we
need to know that, for each
\rho\in\mathsf{SetEnv}
,
T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}
is
an
\omega
-cocontinuous endofunctor on
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
, and thus
admits a fixpoint. Since
T_{H,\rho}^{\mathsf{Set}}
is defined in terms of

\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
, this means that
interpretations of types must be such functors, which in turn means
that the actions of set interpretations of types on objects and on
morphisms in
\mathsf{SetEnv}
are intertwined. Fortunately, we know
from
[
JP19
]
that, for every
\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G
,

\llbracket{\Gamma;\overline{\alpha}\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
is actually in
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]

where
k=|\overline{\alpha}|
. This means that for each
\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi^{k},\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
, the corresponding operator

T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H}
can be extended to a
functor
from
\mathsf{SetEnv}
to

[[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}],[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]]
. The action of
T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H}
on an object

\rho\in\mathsf{SetEnv}
is given by the higher-order functor

T_{H,\rho}^{\mathsf{Set}}
, whose actions on objects (functors in
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
) and morphisms (natural transformations) between them are
given in Definition
3.1
. Its action on a morphism
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
is the higher-order natural transformation

T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f}:T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}\to T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}}
whose action on

F:[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
is the natural transformation
T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f}\,F:T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}\,F\to T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}}\,F
whose component at

\overline{A}
is
(T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f}\,F)_{\overline{A}}=\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f[\phi:=\mathit{id}_{F}][\overline{\alpha:=\mathit{id}_{A}}]
.
Note that this action of
T_{H,\rho}^{\mathsf{Set}}
on morphisms appears in the
final clause of Definition
3.1
below. It will
indeed be well-defined there because the functorial action of

\llbracket{\Gamma;\phi,\overline{\alpha}\vdash H}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}
will already be available
from the induction hypothesis on
H
.

Using
T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H}
, we can define the functorial action of set
interpretation.

{defi}

Let
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
be a morphism between set environments
\rho

and
\rho^{\prime}
(so that
\rho|_{\mathbb{T}}=\rho^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{T}}
). The action

\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f
of
\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}

on
f
is given by:

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{0}
then
\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{0}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f=\mathit{id}_{0}

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{1}
then
\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\mathbb{1}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f=\mathit{id}_{1}

•

If
\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G
then

\llbracket{\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f=\mathit{id}_{\llbracket{\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash\mathsf{Nat}^{\overline{\alpha}}\,F\,G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}
then

\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f:\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho\to\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}=(\rho\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}\to(\rho^{\prime}\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}

is defined by

\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash\phi\overline{F}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f=(f\phi)_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}}\,\circ\,(\rho\phi){\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}=(\rho^{\prime}\phi){\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}\,\circ\,(f\phi)_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}}

The latter equality holds because
\rho\phi
and

\rho^{\prime}\phi
are functors and
f\phi:\rho\phi\to\rho^{\prime}\phi
is a
natural transformation, so the following naturality square commutes:

\begin{CD}(\rho\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}@>{(f\phi)_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}}}>{}>(\rho^{\prime}\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}\\
@V{(\rho\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}V{}V@V{(\rho^{\prime}\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}V{}V\\
(\rho\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}@>{(f\phi)_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}}}>{}>(\rho^{\prime}\phi)\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}\end{CD}

(1)

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G
then
\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f
is defined by

\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f(\mathsf{inl}\,x)=\mathsf{inl}\,(\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}fx)

and
\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F+G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f(\mathsf{inr}\,y)=\mathsf{inr}\,(\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}fy)

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F\times G
then

\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F\times G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f=\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash F}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f\times\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f

•

If
\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{G}
then

\begin{array}[]{lll}\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{G}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f&:&\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{G}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho\to\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash(\mu\phi.\lambda\overline{\alpha}.H)\overline{G}}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}\\
&=&(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}\to(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}\end{array}

is
defined by

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f})_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}}\circ(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}\\
=&(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}\circ(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f})_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}}\end{array}

The latter equality holds because
\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho}
and
\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}}
are functors and
\mu T_{H,f}^{\mathsf{Set}}:\mu T_{H,\rho}^{\mathsf{Set}}\to\mu T_{H,\rho^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{Set}}
is a
natural transformation, so the following naturality square commutes:

\begin{CD}(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}@>{(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f})_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}}}>{}>(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho}\\
@V{(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}V{}V@V{(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}f}}V{}V\\
(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}@>{(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,f})_{\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}}}>{}>(\mu T^{\mathsf{Set}}_{H,\rho^{\prime}})\overline{\llbracket{\Gamma;\Phi\vdash G}\rrbracket^{\mathsf{Set}}\rho^{\prime}}\end{CD}

(2)

Definitions
3.1
and
3.1
respect
weakening, i.e., ensure that a type and its weakenings have the same
set interpretations.

3.2.
Interpreting Types as Relations

{defi}

A
k
-ary relation transformer

F
is a triple
(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})
,
where

•

F^{1},F^{2}:[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
and
F^{*}:[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
are
functors

•

If
R_{1}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,R_{k}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{k},B_{k})
, then
F^{*}\overline{R}:\mathsf{Rel}(F^{1}\overline{A},F^{2}\overline{B})

•

If
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k})\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{k},S_{k})
then
F^{*}\overline{(\alpha,\beta)}=(F^{1}\overline{\alpha},F^{2}\overline{\beta})

We define
F\overline{R}
to be
F^{*}\overline{R}
and

F\overline{(\alpha,\beta)}
to be
F^{*}\overline{(\alpha,\beta)}
.

The last clause of Definition
3.2
expands to: if

\overline{(a,b)\in R}
implies
\overline{(\alpha\,a,\beta\,b)\in S}
then

(c,d)\in F^{*}\overline{R}
implies
(F^{1}\overline{\alpha}\,c,F^{2}\overline{\beta}\,d)\in F^{*}\overline{S}
. When convenient we identify a
0
-ary relation
transformer
(A,B,R)
with
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
, and write
\pi_{1}F
for

F^{1}
and
\pi_{2}F
for
F^{2}
. Below we extend these conventions to
relation environments in the obvious ways.

{defi}

The category
RT_{k}
of
k
-ary relation transformers is given by the
following data:

•

An object of
RT_{k}
is a relation transformer

•

A morphism
\delta:(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\to(G^{1},G^{2},G^{*})
in
RT_{k}

is a pair of natural transformations
(\delta^{1},\delta^{2})
, where

\delta^{1}:F^{1}\to G^{1}
and
\delta^{2}:F^{2}\to G^{2}
, such that,
for all
\overline{R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)}
, if
(x,y)\in F^{*}\overline{R}
then

(\delta^{1}_{\overline{A}}x,\delta^{2}_{\overline{B}}y)\in G^{*}\overline{R}

•

Identity morphisms and composition are inherited from the
category of functors on
\mathsf{Set}

{defi}

A
higher-order relation transformer

H
on
RT_{k}
is a triple
H=(H^{1},H^{2},H^{*})
, where

•

H^{1}
and
H^{2}
are functors from
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
to
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]

•

H^{*}
is a functor from
RT_{k}
to
[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]

•

H^{*}(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\overline{R}:\mathsf{Rel}(H^{1}F^{1}\overline{A},H^{2}F^{2}\overline{B})

whenever
R_{1}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,R_{k}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{k},B_{k})

•

H^{*}(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\,\overline{(\alpha,\beta)}=(H^{1}F^{1}\overline{\alpha},H^{2}F^{2}\overline{\beta})
whenever
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k})

\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{k},S_{k})

•

For all
\overline{R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)}
,

\pi_{1}((H^{*}(\delta^{1},\delta^{2}))_{\overline{R}})=(H^{1}\delta^{1})_{\overline{A}}
and

\pi_{2}((H^{*}(\delta^{1},\delta^{2}))_{\overline{R}})=(H^{2}\delta^{2})_{\overline{B}}

Definition
3.2
entails that every higher-order
relation transformer
H
is an endofunctor on
RT_{k}
, where

•

The action of
H
on objects is given by
H\,(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})=(H^{1}F^{1},\,H^{2}F^{2},\,H^{*}(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*}))

•

The action of
H
on morphisms is given by

H\,(\delta^{1},\delta^{2})=(H^{1}\delta^{1},H^{2}\delta^{2})
for

(\delta^{1},\delta^{2}):(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\to(G^{1},G^{2},G^{*})

Note that the last condition of Definition
3.2

entails that if
(\delta^{1},\delta^{2}):(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\to(G^{1},G^{2},G^{*})

in
RT_{k}
, if
R_{1}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,R_{k}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{k},B_{k})
, and if
(x,y)\in H^{*}(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\overline{R}
, then

((H^{1}\delta^{1})_{\overline{A}}x,(H^{2}\delta^{2})_{\overline{B}}y)\in H^{*}(G^{1},G^{2},G^{*})\overline{R}

{defi}

A
morphism

\sigma:H\to K
between higher-order relation
transformers
H
and
K
on
RT_{k}
is a pair
\sigma=(\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2})
, where
\sigma^{1}:H^{1}\to K^{1}
and
\sigma^{2}:H^{2}\to K^{2}
are natural transformations between endofunctors on

[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
such that
((\sigma^{1}_{F^{1}})_{\overline{A}},(\sigma^{2}_{F^{2}})_{\overline{B}})
is a morphism from
H^{*}F\overline{R}
to
K^{*}F\overline{R}
in
\mathsf{Rel}
for any
F\in RT_{k}

and any
k
-tuple of relations
\overline{R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)}
.

Definition
3.2
entails that a morphism
\sigma

between higher-order relation transformers is a natural transformation
between endofunctors on
RT_{k}
whose component at
F=(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\in RT_{k}
is given by
\sigma_{F}=(\sigma^{1}_{F^{1}},\sigma^{2}_{F^{2}})
.
Moreover,
\sigma^{i}_{F^{i}}
is natural in
F^{i}:[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
, and,
for every
F=(F^{1},F^{2},F^{*})\in RT_{k}
, both

(\sigma^{1}_{F^{1}})_{\overline{A}}
and

(\sigma^{2}_{F^{2}})_{\overline{A}}
are natural in
\overline{A}
.

Critically, we can compute
\omega
-directed colimits in

RT_{k}
. Indeed, if
\cal D
is an
\omega
-directed set then
{{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{d\in{\cal D}}{(F^{1}_{d},F^{2}_{d},F^{*}_{d})}}=({{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{d\in{\cal D}}{F^{1}_{d}}},{{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{d\in{\cal D}}{F^{2}_{d}}},{{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{d\in{\cal D}}{F^{*}_{d}}})
. We define a higher-order relation transformer
T=(T^{1},T^{2},T^{*})
on
RT_{k}
to be
\omega
-cocontinuous
if
T^{1}

and
T^{2}
are
\omega
-cocontinuous endofunctors on
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]

and
T^{*}
is an
\omega
-cocontinuous functor from
RT_{k}
to

[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
, i.e., is in
[RT_{k},[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]]
.
Now, for any
k
, any
A:\mathsf{Set}
, and any
R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)
, let

K^{\mathsf{Set}}_{A}
be the constantly
A
-valued functor from
\mathsf{Set}^{k}
to

\mathsf{Set}
and
K^{\mathsf{Rel}}_{R}
be the constantly
R
-valued functor from

\mathsf{Rel}^{k}
to
\mathsf{Rel}
. Also let
0
denote either the initial object of
either
\mathsf{Set}
or
\mathsf{Rel}
, as appropriate. Observing that, for every

k
,
K^{\mathsf{Set}}_{0}
is initial in
[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]
, and
K^{\mathsf{Rel}}_{0}
is
initial in
[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
, we have that, for each
k
,
K_{0}=(K^{\mathsf{Set}}_{0},K^{\mathsf{Set}}_{0},K^{\mathsf{Rel}}_{0})
is initial in
RT_{k}
. Thus, if
T=(T^{1},T^{2},T^{*}):RT_{k}\to RT_{k}
is a higher-order relation transformer
on
RT_{k}
then we can define the relation transformer
\mu T
to be

{{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{T^{n}K_{0}}}
. It is not hard to see that
\mu T
is
given explicitly as

\mu T=(\mu T^{1},\mu T^{2},{{\underrightarrow{\lim}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{(T^{n}K_{0})^{*}}})

(3)

Moreover,
\mu T
really is a fixpoint for
T
if
T
is

\omega
-cocontinuous:

Lemma 1
.

For any
T:[RT_{k},RT_{k}]
,
\mu T\cong T(\mu T)
.

The isomorphism is given by the morphisms
(\mathit{in}_{1},\mathit{in}_{2}):T(\mu T)\to\mu T
and
(in_{1}^{-1},in_{2}^{-1}):\mu T\to T(\mu T)
in
RT_{k}
. The latter is always a morphism in
RT_{k}
,
but the former need not be if
T
is not
\omega
-cocontinuous.

It is worth noting that the third component in Equation (
3
)
is the colimit in
[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
of third components of relation
transformers, rather than a fixpoint of an endofunctor on

[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
. There is thus an asymmetry between the first two
components of
\mu T
and its third component, which reflects the
important conceptual observation that the third component of a
higher-order relation transformer on
RT_{k}
need not be a functor on
all of
[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
. In particular, although we can define

T_{H,\rho}\,F
for a relation transformer
F
in
Definition
3.2
below, it is not clear how we could
define it for an arbitrary
F:[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]
.

{defi}

A
relation environment
maps each type variable in
\mathbb{T}^{k}\cup\mathbb{F}^{k}
to a
k
-ary relation transformer. A morphism
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
between relation environments
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
with

\rho|_{\mathbb{T}}=\rho^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{T}}
maps each type constructor variable

\psi^{k}\in\mathbb{T}
to the identity morphism on
\rho\psi^{k}=\rho^{\prime}\psi^{k}
and each functorial variable
\phi^{k}\in\mathbb{F}
to a morphism
from the
k
-ary relation transformer
\rho\phi
to the
k
-ary
relation transformer
\rho^{\prime}\phi
. Composition of morphisms on
relation environments is given componentwise, with the identity
morphism mapping each relation environment to itself. This gives a
category of relation environments and morphisms between them, denoted

\mathsf{RelEnv}
.

We identify a
0
-ary relation transformer with the relation
(transformer) that is its codomain and consider a relation environment
to map a type variable of arity
0
to a relation. We write

\rho[\overline{\alpha:=R}]
for the relation environment
\rho^{\prime}
such
that
\rho^{\prime}\alpha_{i}\,=R_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
and
\rho^{\prime}\alpha=\rho\alpha
if
\alpha\not\in\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}\}
. If
\rho

is a relation environment, we write
\pi_{1}\rho
and
\pi_{2}\rho
for
the set environments mapping each type variable
\phi
to the functors

(\rho\phi)^{1}
and
(\rho\phi)^{2}
, respectively.

{defi}

For each
k
, an
\omega
-cocontinuous environment transformer

H
is a triple
H=(H^{1},H^{2},H^{*})
, where

•

H^{1}
and
H^{2}
are objects in
[\mathsf{SetEnv},[\mathsf{Set}^{k},\mathsf{Set}]]

•

H^{*}
is an object in
[\mathsf{RelEnv},[\mathsf{Rel}^{k},\mathsf{Rel}]]

•

H^{*}\rho\,\overline{R}:\mathsf{Rel}(H^{1}(\pi_{1}\rho)\,\overline{A},H^{2}(\pi_{2}\rho)\,\overline{B})
whenever
R_{1}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,R_{k}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{k},B_{k})

•

H^{*}\rho\,\overline{(\alpha,\beta)}=(H^{1}(\pi_{1}\rho)\,\overline{\alpha},H^{2}(\pi_{2}\rho)\,\overline{\beta})
whenever

(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{1},S_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k})\in\mathsf{Hom_{Rel}}(R_{k},S_{k})

•

For all
\overline{R:\mathsf{Rel}(A,B)}
,
\pi_{1}(H^{*}f\,{\overline{R}})=H^{1}(\pi_{1}f)\,{\overline{A}}
and
\pi_{2}(H^{*}f\,{\overline{R}})=H^{2}(\pi_{2}f)\,{\overline{B}}

Definition
3.2
entails that every

\omega
-cocontinuous environment transformer

is a

\omega
-cocontinuous functor from
\mathsf{RelEnv}
to
RT_{k}
, where

•

The action of
H
on
\rho
in
\mathsf{RelEnv}
is given by
H\rho=(H^{1}(\pi_{1}\rho),\,H^{2}(\pi_{2}\rho),\,H^{*}\rho)

•

The action of
H
on morphisms
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{RelEnv}

is given by
Hf=(H^{1}(\pi_{1}f),H^{2}(\pi_{2}f))

Note that the last condition of Definition
3.2

entails that if
f:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
, if

R_{1}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,R_{k}:\mathsf{Rel}(A_{k},B_{k})
, and if
(x,y)\in H^{*}\rho\,\overline{R}
, then

(H^{1}(\pi_{1}f)\,{\overline{A}}\,x,H^{2}(\pi_{2}f)\,{\overline{B}}\,y)\in H^{*}\rho^{\prime}\,\overline{R}

Considering
\mathsf{RelEnv}
as a subcategory of the product
\Pi_{\phi^{k}\in\mathbb{T}\cup\mathbb{F}}RT_{k}
, computation of
\omega
-directed colimits
in
RT_{k}
extends componentwise to
\mathsf{RelEnv}
. Recalling from the start
of this subsection that Definition
3.2
is given mutually
inductively with Definition
